#6940. Lean Into Chocolate Brown Coats to Look Sumptuous as the Weather Drops

It’s the color of the year, at this point!

It’s been made very clear that chocolate brown is the color of the fall and winter at this point. It’s become a staple I cannot get away from, and I honestly don’t want to. So as we gear up for the remainder of fall, and start preparing for the chilly winter months, I’m tapping into the chocolate brown trend with my outerwear pieces, too.

I’m always on the hunt for a good trench coat, fur jacket, and everything in between once the temperature drops below 50 degrees. So if that’s you too, I’ve got some major style inspiration below for your perusing. Let’s get those winter closets ready because I don’t believe in sacrificing style for warmth.

A quality coat works for an everyday fall look, and this chocolate brown coloring adds depth to what is a simple outfit. Throw one on with your jeans and white top and you’re good to go all day.

Personally, the crewneck sweater is a very underutilized piece in everyone’s closet. It works so well as a layering top, especially during the fall months. Adding this chocolate brown one on top of a checkered button-down top is incredibly stylish and perfect for the season, especially when topped with a textured stole for added warmth.

It’s easy to rely on a black leather jacket because it’s what’s popular, but may I introduce you to the elevated, cooler cousin: a brown leather jacket. It softens your look and brings something different to the table when mapping out your fall outfits.

Whether you’re going to work or just really into business casual, a chocolate brown blazer is such a perfect addition to your closet. It’s polished, but also just a good outerwear staple to have on deck.

I am a huge fan of faux fur coats. They add so much drama to any look and why not tap into that chicness with a brown version? It’s trendy, but can also last for several seasons, never feeling out of style.

#6937. Mark Rober's Class CrunchLabs Courses Now Available for Free on YouTube

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Designed in partnership with the National Science Teaching Association, the standards-aligned "Carnival of Collisions: Contact Forces" and "Mission MagLev: Electric & Magentic Forces" courses offer free video lessons, guided activities, and supplemental teaching resources for grades 6-8.

Class CrunchLabs was launched last year to offer free hands-on science curriculum resources including teaching guides, classroom slides, student readings and handouts, assessments, and instructional videos featuring Rober alongside "Science Bob" Pflugfelder, celebrity guests, and student co-hosts. The initiative was backed by a $7.2 million grant to create the CrunchLabs.org Foundation, a nonprofit "dedicated to revolutionizing science education in a way that promotes critical thinking and inspires lasting curiosity and confidence in STEM," according to a news announcement at the time.

The organization plans to roll out additional courses on YouTube over time, with the ultimate goal of posting more than 1,000 science videos in 34 languages.

#6933. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#6932. Designers Say These Are the 10 Best Antiques to Find at Estate Sales

Thrift stores and flea markets are popular spots for finding unusual vintage pieces, but they’re not the only options. Estate sales let you browse antiques in the very homes where they’ve been used and collected over the years. And since many items haven’t passed through a dealer, they may cost less than similar finds at an antique shop.

That combination makes estate sales a favorite among interior designers. They’re a source of characterful pieces for both client projects and personal collections. Krystal Reinhard, founder and principal designer of Old Soul Design Studio, likes being able to see items in the settings where they’ve long belonged. “It makes it easier to spot quality, scale, and patina,” she says.

For Valerie Helgeson, founder and lead designer at Design Directions, the appeal of estate sales is the fast-paced nature of the hunt. “There’s an immediacy to them that I like. You have to make a decision and trust your instincts,” she shares.

Whether you’re after a bargain, an authentic antique, or the fun of finding an unexpected treasure, estate sales are worth exploring. Ahead, interior designers reveal the 10 antiques they always look for—and explain why each belongs on your estate sale wish list.

Paige Loperfido, founder and principal designer of Decor & More Design Studio, is always game to grab hand-painted porcelain, midcentury ceramics, and antique glassware, like ice buckets and goblets.

“These collectibles bring color, pattern, and personality to kitchens and dining rooms,” she says. “Beyond their practical use, they easily transition into art, with vintage plates mounted on walls for a dynamic display, or grouped on open shelves. In dining spaces, glassware and tableware act as statement pieces, highlighting one-of-a-kind treasures that feel timeless, sophisticated, and thoughtfully curated, adding both charm and character to special gatherings.”

Reinhard calls antique candlestick holders “an instant layer,” with their warmth, height, and “a little romance with a surprisingly big impact for a small spend.” She adds that she often styles them as matched pairs on mantels and consoles, or as loose trios at varying heights on dining tables and coffee tables.

Going into estate sales with an open mind, Helgeson typically finds herself buying something she “could have never imagined looking for.” But frequently, one item tops her wish list, and that’s copper kitchenware. She displays these antique pieces in kitchens alongside other estate sale–found items, such as turned-wood bowls, old cutting boards, and bread bowls.

Pottery is a designer darling, since these pieces add texture and even imperfection (in a good way) to spaces, as Reinhard puts it. She tracks down pottery at estate sales, then showcases it on open shelving, sideboards, and tablescapes. She prefers to pair pottery with “something refined,” like brass, stone, or glass, to keep the mix elevated.

Loperfido’s favorite estate sale gems are what she calls “culturally rich pieces” that have been collected by homeowners from around the world. Think: Chinese antiques like foo dogs and ginger jars, and vintage ceramics such as majolica fish candlesticks, figurative pieces, or midcentury glazed pottery.

Oftentimes, she says that these particular pieces “showcase incredible craftsmanship and character,” adding that they’re perfect to layer on surfaces throughout the house, from bookshelves to nightstands to end tables.

At estate sales, Helgeson is scanning the spaces for “furniture with a strong silhouette.” Acting as anchors in rooms, Helgeson believes in accessorizing the rest of these spaces with vintage finds, which she says makes even new pieces appear like collected treasures.

“Vintage rugs bring color, pattern, and age instantly,” Reinhard remarks. She decorates with them “everywhere”: in powder rooms, mudrooms, and under seating areas to anchor the furniture and add depth.

“Original art can feel inaccessible at gallery pricing, but estate sales are one of the best places to find something truly special for a fraction of the cost,” Reinhard points out. She goes for single statement pieces, small collected clusters, or creates a full gallery wall that combines vintage art with modern pieces and personal photos.

Continually on the lookout for leather-bound collections and cocktail books, Reinhard snaps up antique books at estate sales.

“They’re one of the easiest ways to add personality and story—especially art, history, and travel titles that feel timeless and intentional,” she says. “I use them everywhere—stacked on coffee tables, tucked onto consoles, layered into shelves, or used as risers for objects to create height and visual rhythm.”

Loperfido says that these “finely detailed etched artworks” are often overlooked at estate sales, but they can instantly add sophistication and historical texture to any space.

“Small but rich in visual storytelling, they work beautifully against neutral or patterned backgrounds,” she notes.

#6929. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

#6928. These 7 Storage Solutions for Small Spaces Look Too Good to Hide

Display-worthy baskets, glass-fronted cabinets, and colorful tins can make organization part of your decor.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From a Brooklyn studio to a narrow Baltimore rowhouse, two decades of small-space living have taught me how to make every square foot count. Add in my Type A Virgo tendencies and an appreciation for good design, and I have strong opinions about storage: Namely, it should be as good-looking as it is hard-working. My first stop when hunting for products that deliver just that is always IKEA. The Swedish retailer has an unmatched selection of storage furniture and decor. Here are seven pieces that offer both function and form.

I have never known the pleasure of a linen closet, but I’ve gotten so many compliments on this metal-and-glass cabinet in my bathroom that I no longer mind. The reeded glass offers an unexpected texture while obscuring what’s inside to reduce visual clutter, and each interior shelf is roomy enough to hold a stack of towels, plus a small bin for toiletries and medicine bottles. I like to use the bamboo UPPDATERA boxes for material contrast; the larger style works great on the lower shelves to tame my mess of serums, makeup, lotions, and the like.

IKEA sells a huge variety, but the woven styles like this one are particularly lovely to keep on display. This chic new design features sliding doors—a space-saver itself since you won’t need to account for door clearance—adjustable shelves, and two drawers.

This cabinet hides 14 pairs behind a traditional design, and sticks out from the wall less than a foot. All kitchens can benefit from more storage, but it’s the small cook spaces where you really need to get creative. A mobile cart offers that utility, and this one features three shelves for anything from spices and baking supplies to serveware or a coffee bar.

Another simple small kitchen storage idea: Keep your dry goods in stackable containers, like the BETTHAJ tins, to maximize your pantry shelf space. They’re far easier to handle than flimsy paper containers or plastic bags, plus, they look great on a countertop or open shelving. They come in five different sizes, from 19 oz. to 3.3 qt., so you can mix and match to suit your needs.

Why choose between extra seating or more storage when you can have both? This sturdy little pouf hides a compartment that I use for an extra throw blanket, but it could also fit a few games, your knitting supplies, or remote controls. The cushioned top is taut enough that it can also function as a side table, making this a small space triple threat.

Head to IKEA to find storage that maximizes space without sacrificing style.

#6926. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

#6922. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

#6921. The Best Mini PCs to Buy in 2026

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These high-performing, small-footprint models will clean up your workspace.

Tiny computers are nothing new. I know you have one in your pocket right now—that is, if you’re not reading this on it already. The smartphone proved that computers can be anything, and anything can be a computer. Even putting aside the smartphone and the laptop, desktops have been getting smaller and changing form over time. The mini PC (sometimes also called a NUC) is a term that’s getting a lot of use lately, but it’s for something that’s more or less existed for decades: a small, prebuilt computer.

Traditionally, a PC runs Windows, but you can find Linux-based and MacOS-based mini PCs too. Of course, Apple makes its own in the form of the Mac Mini, probably the most popular one on the market. The original version debuted back in 2005, proving my point that these are nothing new, and has made its way into millions of homes without needing anyone to utter the words “mini PC.”

Point is, if you’re looking for something with the capability of a desktop without wanting a space-hogging tower, this is what you need to be looking at. Whether you need a machine for school, work, or gaming, these are the most capable desktops that come in shockingly small packages. These four machines are the best mini PCs to buy right now.

Starting off, it’s the 2026 Mac Mini. For the average person, you can start and stop the shopping journey here. If you want a compact home computer and you’re already deeply embedded in MacOS and Apple’s ecosystem, it’s hard to resist the simple appeal of the Mac Mini M6. It’s unassuming, smaller than a cable box and about the size of your internet modem. Ninety percent of the time, it’s as quiet as either of those too, despite being a fast mid-range computer optimized for AI workloads (more so than any generation before it). In terms of connections, you get a full stack; three Thunderbolt 4 ports, an HDMI port, and an Ethernet port in the back, as well as two USB-C ports and a headphone jack in front. More than plenty for all your accessories.

Like I said, this is a mid-range Mac. It’s just as suited for a home office as the main family computer in the living room. For something with professional level capabilities (we are all professionals, but I’m talking video editing, software development, any 3D model rendering), you’ll want the one with the M5 Pro chip instead. As always, with either of these, the biggest downside is Apple’s walled garden. You can’t upgrade the RAM or storage capacity like you can with other PCs, and Apple isn’t afraid to charge an arm and a leg for the versions that come with extra.

If you’re in both a time and money crunch, this is the kind of computer you want. For less than $400, it’s a Windows-capable machine with 16 gigs of RAM that can output to up to three 4K displays at a time. And unless you are pushing its processing power, it stays cool and relatively silent. It’s got all the ports you’d want—USB, HDMI, DPI, headphone jack—plus two high-speed USB-A ports in the back. These are decent for when you have large files to transfer, but they admittedly pale in terms of speed compared to a Thunderbolt port.

At the price it’s currently at, this is also cheaper than any gaming console out there. Not even as powerful as a Nintendo Switch (the old one), but it can run Minecraft and other casual or indie titles with little trouble.

If your job or home business requires running more and more local AI models (as many do), you might want a mini PC designed with these workflows in mind. Plus, it has every kind of port you’d want and more. With HDMI, USB, Thunderbolt, Ethernet, even an SD card slot and Copilot button, it’s fully kitted out for even the more niche uses.

It’s not a perfect solution. This machine will struggle with more intensive tasks and you’ll hear about it. For its size and light frame (it’s easily mountable to a monitor stand), though, it’s nothing to sneeze at.

Any machine can run some video games, but if you’re the type who considers gaming your hobby of choice, and you need something to play all the latest releases in 4K, well, then you need something as dedicated as you. For a reasonable price tag (trust me, it is), you’re getting a powerful PC with an RTX 5060 graphics card and 32 gigabytes of RAM. For the completely uninitiated, that’s a mid-range 2025 dedicated graphics card—sure to run any modern game, but when it comes to more demanding titles you just may have to choose to prioritize either frame rate or visuals.

Beyond gaming, this is a capable machine due to the decent GPU. And for those who don’t want to tinker at all, the G1 Pro offers three default modes to quickly swap between. Swap from Office to Gaming when it’s time to play, or go into Beast mode when you’re really trying to throttle the machine and get the most out of it.

The real reason to get something like this over a console or Valve’s Steam Machine is that it’s upgradeable. The storage and RAM can both be swapped out for newer or higher-capacity options. It’s not a full-on PC, though. With the CPU and GPU, you’re stuck with what is on board.

#6918. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

We’ve been testing at-home laser hair removal devices for five years, bringing you the best ones with all of our firsthand experience and updating whenever we try new launches or underrated gems. To compile this full list, we:

Laser hair removal is a process where specific wavelengths of light are directed onto your skin in little pulses, targeting and heating up the hair follicle to destroy a portion of it with each treatment, says dermatologist Arash Akhavan, MD. Compared to in-office treatments (which, although pricey, are done by board-certified derms or trained professionals who know exactly what they’re doing), at-home devices have a much lower energy output—mostly because they often use IPL (intense pulsed light) rather than true laser technology—which makes them safer for you to use on your own, says dermatologist Julie Russak, MD.

Yes, at-home laser-hair removal devices do work, but they take consistency. Because at-home devices use lower energy levels than in-office treatments, they require continued use (think: weekly or every time you shave) to notice similar results, says cosmetic doctor Ana Mansouri, MD. Basically, they are not for the lazy.

No, laser hair removal isn’t necessarily permanent (even though that’s what they told us). Despite how they sound, these devices don’t permanently remove hair in a few sessions, leaving you hairless like a full-body wax. Instead, laser-removal devices only help to reduce the total number of hair follicles in your skin and potentially make your existing hair finer and thinner (only electrolysis hair removal can permanently remove all hair, FYI). Even in-office laser hair removal isn’t completely permanent. Typically, you’ll need to go in once every 8 to 12 weeks after you’ve done your round of treatments (which is usually between 6 to 12, depending on the area, your hair, etc.)

Neither IPL nor laser-hair removal is “better”—it really just comes down to preference. True laser hair removal only uses one wavelength of light (essentially, one precise laser beam) that targets just the hair follicle to help slow down hair growth. IPL (intense pulsed light), on the other hand, isn’t actually a laser. Instead, they emit broad-spectrum light in multiple wavelengths that spread across a larger area of skin, meaning they are less precise and won’t penetrate your skin as deeply. That’s why IPL devices are generally gentler and easier for people to use, which is why most at-home devices use IPL rather than laser beams.

That said, both IPL and laser-hair removal can slowly destroy your hair follicle to reduce hair growth over time with continued use. But expect laser-hair removal to be a bit stronger (and potentially more painful or irritating), which is why it often yields quicker and better results. Meanwhile, IPL devices are usually not as intense, so they’re great for using consistently at home or for use on sensitive skin, like on your bikini line or face.

Do dermatologists recommend at-home laser hair removal?

Yes, it’s okay to do laser-hair removal at home, according to Dr. Akhavan, but there are some things to consider. “At-home devices tend to be generally safe, though a real potential for adverse effects, like scarring, does exist,” he says. Which is why most dermatologists will suggest opting for the professional in-office route, if you have the means (laser hair removal can cost anywhere from $200 to $1,500 and usually requires at least six sessions spaced six weeks apart).

Just note: “If you’re using an at-home device, don’t use it on sensitive areas, like your face and neck,” says Dr. Akhavan. “And if you have sensitive skin, always patch-test first on a smaller area, then wait three to four weeks to make sure there are no complications.” And depending on the area you’re treating, you may also need to adjust your skincare routine to exclude skin-thinning ingredients (like chemical exfoliants or retinol serums) for three days before and after your laser-hair removal treatment.

Does at-home laser hair removal work on dark skin?

Currently, at-home laser-hair removal devices don’t truly work for dark skin. These lasers work by targeting the dark color of your hair follicle, and at-home devices don’t have the technology yet to properly distinguish between dark hair follicles and dark skin, meaning they can potentially burn you. While some devices might market themselves as being safe for dark skin, they are usually considering medium-to-tan skin versus actual deep-brown skin tones.

So far, there are no at-home devices that are safe to use on true type-six Fitzpatrick scale skin tones (reminder: This dermatological scale ranges from one to six, with six being “brown to dark-brown” skin). Most laser-hair removal devices are only approved for up to level-four skin tones, with four being “olive to medium” skin. But if you’re unsure where your skin tone lies, talk to your dermatologist before you try an at-home laser device.

What happens if you don’t shave before at home laser hair removal?

Yes, you should shave about 24 hours before using an at-home laser-hair removal device, otherwise the IPL/laser won’t be able to correctly target the melanin (pigment) in your hair follicle. This could potentially cause discomfort, like redness, swelling, and irritation. Because these devices work by homing in on dark, contrasting colors (think: a black dot on a white canvas), they need to be able to differentiate between your skin and your follicle to prevent errors or burns. FYI: Even stubble isn't okay for laser hair removal because it will create too much heat and can damage your skin (plus, it won’t even work to help reduce the hair anyway).

Just remember: Don’t wax before your laser hair removal. Waxing completely rips out the hair and its root, which will leave nothing for the light to target, and you won’t be able to use your device. Instead, make sure your skin is freshly shaved and free of lotions or creams.

At-home laser hair removal can be slightly painful for some, but it all depends on your pain tolerance. Many people describe it as a light sting—like a rubber band snapping against your skin—but some people don’t even notice any pain at all. Your device’s strength and intensity levels will also dictate how painful the treatment will be. In general, though, most people say it’s relatively comfortable when done at home (I would give it a 1 out of 10—it’s a piece of cake, IMO).

For the most part, yes, you can use at-home laser hair removal devices on your bikini line. But you should be very careful. This should only ever be done externally on your vulva. And keep in mind how sensitive the skin in your pubic region is. Generally, it's best to opt for the lightest setting, and go slow with the device. Of all of the at-home IPL devices we tested, our favorite for pubic hair was the NOOD Flasher Pro.

It's best to skip lasering until your skin has cleared if you're dealing with razor bumps. Reminder: Razor bumps are the tiny red bumps you get on your skin after shaving or waxing when the hair strand grows back into the skin instead of out. They're usually inflamed and irritated, which is why it's ideal to just let them heal and swipe on a soothing treatment for a few days before lasering.

If you’re just starting out with at-home laser hair removal, there can be a slight learning curve. Keep these in mind:

Like I said above, you have to shave beforehand to see any reduction in hair, and it’s usually recommended to shave right before. Usually, I’ll exfoliate in the shower the day before with a body scrub, then 24 hours later, I’ll shave and use my laser-hair removal device on clean, dry skin. Exfoliating the day before helps cut down on some irritation but still ensures a close shave.

While laser hair removal is typically well-tolerated, it can potentially make your skin a little inflamed and irritated. To offset this, always finish your treatments with a moisturizing body treatment, whether that’s a body lotion, body serum, or rich body butter. And if you’re prone to ingrown hairs, try incorporating an exfoliating treatment once or twice a week, like a chemical exfoliant pad or serum.

Remember: Laser hair removal takes time. One treatment won’t do much, and neither will a couple. It takes up to three months to start noticing a reduction in hair. Stay consistent (this usually means treating your skin once a week, but it depends on your skin type).

Only some devices are suitable for deep skin tones, and even fewer can work on light hair, so read the description of the product carefully, then scan the reviews for people who have a similar skin tone or hair color as you and see how they rate the device. We noted above if a device can be used on lighter hair, fyi.

You’ll also want to consider the size of the device you’re purchasing and the area that you intend to use it on, says Dr. Mansouri. Basically, a tiny device meant for your face probably isn’t going to be the best if you’re looking for a tool to quickly target your legs.

Do you want a device you can take on the go? Then you should definitely opt for something cordless (The only one we tested that we loved was by Tria Beauty, FYI). Or if you want something that’s going to be really easy to use, try the Braun Skin-I Expert, which connects to an app to help you track your treatments. Another loved feature by testers? A cooling effect to help bring down potential discomfort and pain, like the Ulike IPL device.

#6914. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6912. A Straight Razor Will Give You the Best Shave of Your Life. Here Are 6 Great Options to Buy.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

If you get razor bumps and ingrowns, don’t be afraid to go old-school.

We’re 120 years past the invention of the safety razor and 50 years past the invention of the cartridge razor. So yes, we understand how annoying it is to even suggest that you should shave with the thing that’s even older—technically as old as shaving itself. Go ahead, make fun of the old-school dandy men’s magazine saying you should be straight-razor shaving with a shaving cream that requires a boar hair brush and smells of tobacco. But to turn shaving from a chore to something you look forward to, well, the straight razor is a one-way ticket to hobby town.

Using an electric razor or beard trimmer is great for getting rid of scruff, but if you ever go for a clean shave, a straight razor is the best tool—full stop. They are the best for your face, don’t give you as many ingrown hairs, and (unlike the five-bladed cartridge razors) don’t get clogged up with hairs after two shaves. Plus they add a sense of elegance to your everyday routine, not to mention a bit of danger, à la Sweeney Todd. Only joking, of course.

So sure, challenge our claim that straight-razor shaving is as good as it gets. Or better yet, try it for a little bit and then tell us we’re wrong. If you clean shave and care about grooming, consider picking up a straight razor and doing it the old-fashioned way.

Frankly, most of these straight razors aren’t even technically straight razors. They are shavettes; hybrids with disposable blades so you don’t have to worry about honing and sharpening (and all the hazards that come with failing to do so). You still get the straight razor vibe, but it’s safer and more convenient. For a starter shavette, you can pick up the Parker SRX straight edge. It’s compatible universally with any double edge blades, so you won’t ever run out.

Take care of this stainless steel beauty and it will retain that sheen for years without a sign of rust. This all makes it a great, affordable pick for intermediate shavers who prioritize performance. For a more gateway razor, I’d pick the Dovo shavette

Fundamentally, this is the same thing as the previous Parker razor. It’s a shavette that uses double-edge disposable blades, so the experience of using it and the accompanying learning curve are the exact same. The difference is the wood handle. It looks great, but if you get anything other than shaving cream on there, it’s certainly not going to be as easy to clean as stainless steel. I still wouldn’t recommend a novice pick this up, but that’s what the safety razor is for.

If you are genuinely just curious about this whole thing, you don’t need to drop even $30 on something you may use once and then put down forever. You can pick up a Classic Samurai razor from Amazon for less than 15 bucks. It’s not the best ever, and you won’t be showing off to your friends, but it’s a way to get your feet wet. Plus it comes with enough extra (safety-razor-compatible) blades that if you do find the knack for straight-razor shaving, you can upgrade to a Parker or Dovo and have plenty of blades to use.

The straight handle is your less flexible, slightly sturdier alternative to your folding shavette. This Feather razor is Japanese made with a straight grip meant to give more fine tuning and control to the average shaver. I only wish that, like Parker shavettes, it was compatible with all double-blade replacements—not just the Feather branded ones.

Whether straight handles are actually easier to control and safer than folding ones is up for debate. Barbers have their preferences and if you are looking to start shaving with a straight razor at home, so will you. If you’ve tried the folding shavettes and can’t quite get a handle (sorry for the pun) on them, this one is worth a shot.

As we exit the shavette category, I turn once again to Dovo. If you want a razor that looks and feels as traditional as it gets while still being brand new, you need a Dovo.

This isn’t for beginners or really even intermediate shavers. If anything, it’s more of a novelty. If you want to use it for real, you should probably be a real barber. If you have the skills (and the disposable income), this will be something fun to whip out for your favorite clients in need of the cleanest shave of their life.

There’s something uniquely satisfying about buying vintage. These come from the era when these were the only razors men used to shave. In a way, that’s how they know they are quality. Just old-fashioned stainless steel blades and a design that modern “retro” can’t come close to replicating.

You can buy these (one per package despite the website’s imagery) sealed or opened and cleaned Friedrich Schlemper straight razors for less than the price of a Dovo. These Sistrum razors are German-made carbon steel, likely dating back to sometime between the 1950s and the 1970s.

Classic Straight Razor: Traditionally, a straight razor has one steel (typically stainless or carbon) blade. In the old days, before the safety and cartridge razors we know and love, this was the only option. Barbers had to hone and sharpen their blades using a leather strap—and if you go classic this is still a requirement. This makes a straight razor an investment that can go south if you don’t take care of it properly. Serious professionals only—it’s not easy to learn how to shave safely with one of these. There’s a reason that as a society, we don’t use them anymore.

Shavettes: This modern hybrid of the safety razor and straight razor is what I recommend over the classic style. They are much more affordable, for starters, and the replaceable blades are much safer, especially for at-home experimentation. If you are a casual looking into straight razors, I cannot stress enough to start and end with a shavette.

Folding vs Straight Handle: In terms of shavettes, it all comes down to preference. A straight handle can be preferable to a lighter, airy folding blade handle due to better ergonomics and greater control. But others find a folding handle to allow them better control and stability. It’s all about whichever you feel allows you more precision while leading to fewer errors.

Esquire has been helping men look (and be) at their best since the heyday of the straight razor. We lived through it. If 90 years of institutional knowledge isn’t going to convince you, nothing will.

#6910. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

#6907. I Went to the World's Biggest Furniture Trade Show—These 6 Trends Are About to Be Everywhere

The furniture profiles, patterns, and palettes you’re about to see everywhere.

Twice a year, High Point, North Carolina, becomes the home industry’s giant showroom, where furniture and design brands unveil new collections for retailers, interior designers, and buyers to see and source. At House Beautiful, we’re your boots on the ground, looking for the ideas that may travel from showroom to store—and into your home next.

This fall, Market made a case for decorating with more character and muddier hues: green moved from the forest floor to the showroom floor, florals claimed entire sofas, and upholstery played with everything from a flash of leg to a full fabric wrap. Add wood-backed furniture as an accent, garden-inspired lighting, and a generous swish of fringe, and the message was clear: The details are ready to take center stage.

This year, green was the unofficial color of Market. Benjamin Moore and Behr had already put the hue on the radar with their 2027 Colors of the Year: Webster Green, a confident mid-tone balancing blue’s coolness with yellow’s warmth, and Grounded, a mossy olive softened by earthy brown undertones. Then came the showroom echoes. Versions of green, from earthy vegetable shades to deeper, moodier tones, appeared nearly everywhere. Kathy Kuo even created a “Music Room” (shown in our opening image) for Rowe Furniture inspired by the verdant shade. The appeal is easy to understand: Green brings the natural world indoors, lending a sense of calm and grounding a space without draining it of personality.

The design industry’s obsession with prints is now a love letter to botanicals. Florals erupted across Market in a myriad of scales, from small, almost sun-bleached botanicals to overscaled, tapestry-adjacent blooms. Even the tiniest prints made a surprisingly bold statement when carried across an entire sofa; the larger blooms brought the feel of a faded heirloom into full view. Both have that grandmotherly, another-era quality, but the impact is anything but timid. Yes, it’s a major design commitment, but why play it safe with a solid, barely-there pattern? It may not be for the faint of design, but we are all in a sofa fully dressed in flowers.

Here’s a furniture detail that changes the whole silhouette: Brands are letting wood take over the outside back—or sides—of upholstered seating. Baker’s sofa makes the move hard to miss, with a broad wood shell framing the piece. It’s a little like “inside-out” upholstery, where the outside gets its own treatment, but here the wood gives the piece architectural shape and presence. As floor plans open up and sofas float in family and living rooms, the back is no longer hidden against a wall. It’s part of the view, and part of how the seating area takes shape, making it all the more important that it feels worth looking at.

Upholstery continues to take its cues from fashion, with a focus on mixing silhouettes: Mini skirts that let those gams show; full, ballooning skirts with all the best parts of an ’80s prom dress; and what we lovingly call body-con upholstery, where the entire chair is wrapped head to toe in one fabric.

The appeal goes beyond a good fashion metaphor, though. Each treatment changes the chair’s proportions: a flash of leg keeps it light, a generous skirt adds volume, and an all-over wrap turns both seat and base into one continuous form, with no leg finish to choose or worry about. Each has its own distinct style POV, making your shopping choices that much more complicated, but in the best way.

If garden motifs aren’t your thing, don’t look up. The floral revival isn’t limited to upholstery and wallpaper; it’s reaching the ceiling, too. Visual Comfort & Co.’s Fairoaks collection, designed by Fisher Weisman, includes a chandelier, sconce, and mini flush mount. Sculptural branches and twigs cleverly disguise the chandelier’s ceiling cord, preserving the romance of the design. At Hudson Valley Lighting, a tulip-inspired chandelier brings a more literal bloom overhead. Both offer a way to bring the garden indoors through shape and silhouette, without committing an entire room to pattern.

Sometimes, all it takes is a little bullion fringe. The thick, tightly twisted cords fall in a dense, weighty row, adding plush texture and a satisfying swish to a skirted chair or sofa. It nods to the Victorian parlor, but stops short of period-drama excess; against a cleaner silhouette or in a spare room, the old-world trim feels playful and unexpectedly fresh. Consider it the design equivalent of a great pair of earrings: a little extra, in exactly the right place.

#6906. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

#6905. Before & After: How an Artist and Architect Couple Turned the “Worst House” on the Block Into Their Dream Home

In Brooklyn, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier transform a crumbling townhouse with equal parts drama and restraint.

Before they stepped foot inside the crumbling brownstone on a landmark block in Brooklyn’s Boerum Hill, artist Katherine Keltner and architect Douglas Gauthier had toured 30 homes and browsed more than 200 online. Neither of them expected to become instantly captivated by the 1869 Italianate townhouse, which was being sold in a stately yet dilapidated condition, having once served as a single-room occupancy (SRO) residence. But it happened nonetheless.

“This was the first one that had the bones that Katherine wanted,” recalls Gauthier, who runs the firm GAuthier Architects. “It follows the classic real estate rule of buying the worst house on the best block.”

The strategy paid off: Once their offer was accepted, the couple embarked on an 18-month-long odyssey that saw them jump through a series of hoops (preservationists; concerned neighbors; skeptical engineers) to completely transform the moldering pile into a creative haven for them to live, work, and raise their daughter.

A true joint-effort, Gauthier oversaw a modest two-story rear expansion and structural alterations, while Keltner took charge of spatial arrangements as well as color, pattern, texture, and artwork—much of it her own.

Once deteriorating, the interiors now have an easy elegance, with plaster walls, arched passageways, and curved corners that give the soaring ceilings an intimate feel. Pink takes an unexpected starring role, appearing throughout the parlor floor and also in the zellige-clad primary bath, but other adventurous hues like mint green and crimson also feature prominently. Capping it off is a plywood-sheathed office for Gauthier’s practice, allowing the family retreat to double as a place of creative inspiration.

Below, Keltner and Gauthier share their biggest lessons (and some “before” pictures) from the painstaking passion project.

When the townhouse was converted to an SRO, it had been chopped up into at least 13 units and two kitchens, and few of the original decorative elements were still intact. A couple had bought it in the 1960s and made it a family home again and added an art gallery space. But by 2021, when Keltner and Gauthier first saw it, the house was in a state of severe neglect, with peeling paint, crumbling floors, ancient appliances—not to mention a 1,500-pound printing press in the studio (that the couple later preserved). Yet, Keltner and Gauthier saw an opportunity, not to recreate the past, but rather forge their own set of rules.

“The house wasn’t burdened with pristine historic details—our moldings were trashed,” says Gauthier. “There were a few things that we kept and celebrated, but to me, it was like a tabula rasa. We can just go in and make it ours.” Katherine agreed: “I feel like sometimes having to preserve things is more expensive than just starting clean,” she says. “And so I thought that was an advantage for us.”

Not having to be too precious with ornamentation allowed the couple to make a bold statement about crown moldings, eschewing tradition and installing curving corners and coved ceilings instead. Nowhere does the effect have more impact than in the parlor, where Gauthier devised a ceiling light in the shape of a giant oval that casts light upward rather than down. “I really like indirect lighting,” he says.

Crafted out of laser-cut plywood, the elliptical fixture is an abstracted homage to the ceiling medallions found in neighboring historic brownstones. “Some of them are incredibly ornate and beautiful, but they don't really have any use—they’re just decoration,” says Gauthier. “Adding ambient lighting to the living space takes that historical reference in pocket.”

Once they were through the process of changing the structure’s zoning from an SRO to a single-family home, Gauthier and Keltner had to contend with its landmarked status, meaning a series of approvals were required for any additions or exterior modifications.

The couple originally planned to construct a three-story extension in the back, but they ended up compromising on two floors. This expansion includes a bump-out for the parlor floor’s kitchen, leading to a rear deck that steps down to the garden. On top, a private terrace connects to the primary bath. “Instead of having that third story, we now have a deck off of our primary suite,” says Keltner. “It’s kind of amazing,” adds Gauthier.

One decision the couple wrestled with mightily was whether to replace the original, 150-year-old pine flooring with ebony-stained white oak. “The floors were part of the reason we liked the house so much,” says Gauthier. But their contractor talked them out of keeping them. “It’s a lot of extra work for them,” says Keltner. “They have to take it up, save it, put something else down, and then put it back. I think we reached a point in time and money where we were like, Okay, fine.”

Another flooring regret was not adding underfoot heating. “I was pushing the mechanical engineers to do radiant heating and radiant cooling,” says Gauthier. “Radiant heat is really lovely—you have warmth under your feet all the time.” But the mechanics behind the system proved complicated and technically redundant, as the home was already configured with a forced-air HVAC system.

“In doing research and other projects, it’s clear that the cost-to-pleasure ratio would have been worth it,” he says now. “We regret not putting the warm floors in.”

While windows might sometimes be an afterthought, Keltner and Gauthier have advice: Go with triple-glazed. “Windows provide heat and cool insulation as well as security,” explains Keltner. “And they can define both the outside and the inside of a space.” The couple went with triple-glazed windows from Austrian-based firm Bewiso that come pre-wired for easy connection to alarm systems. “They’re almost like hurricane windows,” she says.

“You can't break through three panes of glass,” says Gauthier. Due to the building’s landmark status, they had to use historic-style windows on the front facade. “We were able to get these old-fashioned type of windows that when you close them, they feel like you're closing a Mercedes,” says Gauthier. “They completely silence the outdoors.”

The final piece of advice is perhaps the most crucial: “Hire the best contractor you can afford,” says Gauthier.

“The typical advice is to get a few bids and throw out the lowest and the highest,” says Keltner. “People usually take the second lowest.” However, that could be the determining factor between an on-time project and one that drags. “You’ll end up paying the difference both in money and time,” she says. “It’s often hard for people to bite the bullet and go with the slightly higher price, but it really pays off.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.
